========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main reason is… I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to focus on. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s always a stack of essays or some curriculum document to get through, and I’ll sit down genuinely meaning to work on it.

But then ten minutes later I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three completely different things, and I haven’t really taken in any of what’s in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.

Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.

And at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.

I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.

Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.

But if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.

It’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.

It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.

But strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is… I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to focus on. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s always a stack of essays or some curriculum document to get through, and I’ll sit down genuinely meaning to work on it.\n\nBut then ten minutes later I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three completely different things, and I haven’t really taken in any of what’s in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Like, with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down meaning to properly get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’m still on the same page and I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, three completely different things.\n\nIt’s not even always that I dislike the work—though marking can be a bit grim—but it’s like my brain slips off it. Sorry, what was the exact question? Whether it’s all tasks? I suppose I notice it most with the stuff where I have to sit and absorb something for a while.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work or in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. At least, no one’s really said that to me. I might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s different from normal conversation. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.

I mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.

My parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.

It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”

At work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.

And at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is… I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to focus on. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s always a stack of essays or some curriculum document to get through, and I’ll sit down genuinely meaning to work on it.\n\nBut then ten minutes later I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three completely different things, and I haven’t really taken in any of what’s in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Like, with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down meaning to properly get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’m still on the same page and I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, three completely different things.\n\nIt’s not even always that I dislike the work—though marking can be a bit grim—but it’s like my brain slips off it. Sorry, what was the exact question? Whether it’s all tasks? I suppose I notice it most with the stuff where I have to sit and absorb something for a while.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work or in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. At least, no one’s really said that to me. I might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s different from normal conversation. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…\n\nSorry. Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, the missed follow-through has become serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan, and at home my wife has started taking over bills and reminders because she can’t count on me to keep track of them.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.

She’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.

And work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.

It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Like, with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down meaning to properly get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’m still on the same page and I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, three completely different things.\n\nIt’s not even always that I dislike the work—though marking can be a bit grim—but it’s like my brain slips off it. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is… I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to focus on. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s always a stack of essays or some curriculum document to get through, and I’ll sit down genuinely meaning to work on it.\n\nBut then ten minutes later I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three completely different things, and I haven’t really taken in any of what’s in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Like, with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down meaning to properly get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’m still on the same page and I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, three completely different things.\n\nIt’s not even always that I dislike the work—though marking can be a bit grim—but it’s like my brain slips off it. Sorry, what was the exact question? Whether it’s all tasks? I suppose I notice it most with the stuff where I have to sit and absorb something for a while.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work or in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.

Sometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is… I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to focus on. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s always a stack of essays or some curriculum document to get through, and I’ll sit down genuinely meaning to work on it.\n\nBut then ten minutes later I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three completely different things, and I haven’t really taken in any of what’s in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Like, with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down meaning to properly get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’m still on the same page and I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, three completely different things.\n\nIt’s not even always that I dislike the work—though marking can be a bit grim—but it’s like my brain slips off it. Sorry, what was the exact question? Whether it’s all tasks? I suppose I notice it most with the stuff where I have to sit and absorb something for a while.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work or in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. At least, no one’s really said that to me. I might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s different from normal conversation. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…\n\nSorry. Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.\n\nShe’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. I think it’s more the paying attention side of it, yeah. I mean, not that I’m brilliantly organized or anything — my desk at school can get a bit… archaeological — but the thing that really made me come in is that I can sit down with essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work, and then within about ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page.

And I’ll be thinking about three other things, like some email I need to send or something a student said earlier, and then I notice I’ve barely taken in any of what’s actually in front of me. So I’m not sure it’s really impulsivity or juggling loads of things at once. It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.

Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.\n\nShe’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're easily distracted by other things around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less boredom, maybe? I mean, some of the stuff is boring, sure — marking essays isn’t exactly thrilling — but I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and then within ten minutes I realise I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s like I’m thinking about three other things at once, not even always because something in the room distracted me. Sometimes nothing has happened, really. I’m just… not taking in what’s right in front of me, and then I sort of come back and realise I’ve lost the thread completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more because you have trouble paying attention or because you have trouble with multiple things at once, like organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention side of it, yeah. I mean, not that I’m brilliantly organized or anything — my desk at school can get a bit… archaeological — but the thing that really made me come in is that I can sit down with essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work, and then within about ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’ll be thinking about three other things, like some email I need to send or something a student said earlier, and then I notice I’ve barely taken in any of what’s actually in front of me. So I’m not sure it’s really impulsivity or juggling loads of things at once. It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel a need to be constantly active, like you can't slow down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say I feel like I physically can’t slow down, not in a… pacing-round-the-room sort of way.

I mean, teaching does keep you on your feet a lot, but that’s just the job, isn’t it? You’re always going from one classroom thing to another, and then someone knocks on the door about a trip form or a printer not working — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I suppose I haven’t really noticed a constant need to be active like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I’d have to think about what you mean by “can’t slow down.”

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is… I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to focus on. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s always a stack of essays or some curriculum document to get through, and I’ll sit down genuinely meaning to work on it.\n\nBut then ten minutes later I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three completely different things, and I haven’t really taken in any of what’s in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to avoid it—well, sometimes marking is dull, obviously—but it feels like my mind just wanders off before I’ve even properly started.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen with all tasks, or are there certain things you can concentrate on without your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the things that need steady attention, I think. Like, with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down meaning to properly get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’m still on the same page and I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, three completely different things.\n\nIt’s not even always that I dislike the work—though marking can be a bit grim—but it’s like my brain slips off it. Sorry, what was the exact question? Whether it’s all tasks? I suppose I notice it most with the stuff where I have to sit and absorb something for a while.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work or in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s become most… visible, I suppose. I’ve missed follow-through on things enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan, which was pretty humiliating, honestly. It’s not that I don’t care, I just lose track of what I’ve said I’ll do.\n\nAnd at home too—my wife has basically started taking over bills and reminders, because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds awful when I say it out loud. I mean, she’s very organized anyway—she has this whole calendar system with colours and everything, and I always thought, “Oh, that’s just her thing,” but lately it’s more like she has to do it because I keep dropping things. So… yes, both work and home.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’m a particularly impulsive person in the dramatic sense—like, I’m not off doing wild things without thinking. I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. At least, no one’s really said that to me. I might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s different from normal conversation. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…\n\nSorry. Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.\n\nShe’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're easily distracted by other things around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less boredom, maybe? I mean, some of the stuff is boring, sure — marking essays isn’t exactly thrilling — but I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and then within ten minutes I realise I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s like I’m thinking about three other things at once, not even always because something in the room distracted me. Sometimes nothing has happened, really. I’m just… not taking in what’s right in front of me, and then I sort of come back and realise I’ve lost the thread completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more because you have trouble paying attention or because you have trouble with multiple things at once, like organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention side of it, yeah. I mean, not that I’m brilliantly organized or anything — my desk at school can get a bit… archaeological — but the thing that really made me come in is that I can sit down with essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work, and then within about ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’ll be thinking about three other things, like some email I need to send or something a student said earlier, and then I notice I’ve barely taken in any of what’s actually in front of me. So I’m not sure it’s really impulsivity or juggling loads of things at once. It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a need to be constantly active, like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say I feel like I physically can’t slow down, not in a… pacing-round-the-room sort of way.\n\nI mean, teaching does keep you on your feet a lot, but that’s just the job, isn’t it? You’re always going from one classroom thing to another, and then someone knocks on the door about a trip form or a printer not working — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I suppose I haven’t really noticed a constant need to be active like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I’d have to think about what you mean by “can’t slow down.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I don’t… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “this lasted weeks and I was depressed” sort of way.

Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in.
