I mean, I do sometimes say yes to things and then later think, “Why did I agree to that?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nActually, at school there are always these little requests—cover this lesson, look over this thing—and I sometimes answer too quickly just to get the conversation over with, then I realize I haven’t thought about whether I actually have time. But… sorry, I’m drifting a bit. If you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t think that’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think of myself as especially restless, like I’m not pacing around the room or anything. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. At least, no one’s really said that to me. I might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s different from normal conversation. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…\n\nSorry. Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.\n\nShe’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're easily distracted by other things around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less boredom, maybe? I mean, some of the stuff is boring, sure — marking essays isn’t exactly thrilling — but I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and then within ten minutes I realise I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s like I’m thinking about three other things at once, not even always because something in the room distracted me. Sometimes nothing has happened, really. I’m just… not taking in what’s right in front of me, and then I sort of come back and realise I’ve lost the thread completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more because you have trouble paying attention or because you have trouble with multiple things at once, like organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention side of it, yeah. I mean, not that I’m brilliantly organized or anything — my desk at school can get a bit… archaeological — but the thing that really made me come in is that I can sit down with essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work, and then within about ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’ll be thinking about three other things, like some email I need to send or something a student said earlier, and then I notice I’ve barely taken in any of what’s actually in front of me. So I’m not sure it’s really impulsivity or juggling loads of things at once. It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a need to be constantly active, like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say I feel like I physically can’t slow down, not in a… pacing-round-the-room sort of way.\n\nI mean, teaching does keep you on your feet a lot, but that’s just the job, isn’t it? You’re always going from one classroom thing to another, and then someone knocks on the door about a trip form or a printer not working — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I suppose I haven’t really noticed a constant need to be active like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I’d have to think about what you mean by “can’t slow down.”\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant periods of feeling sad, empty, or depressed that lasted for more than a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “this lasted weeks and I was depressed” sort of way.\n\nI mean, I get worn down sometimes, especially around exam periods or when the marking piles up, and then I can feel a bit flat, but that feels more like being tired and overloaded than anything else. Though I suppose it’s hard to tell sometimes, because school terms sort of blur together — you get to the end of a half-term and everyone’s walking around like a ghost, don’t they?\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had a significant spell of feeling sad or empty for weeks like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I think the focus and following through are the main things, really. I wouldn’t say restlessness or impulsivity are equally troubling — at least not what’s bringing me here.

It’s more that I’ll sit down with, say, a stack of essays or some curriculum document, and I genuinely mean to get through it. I’ve cleared the time, I’ve got my coffee, sometimes I’ve even got the nice pen out, which is ridiculous because it makes no difference — and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page and thinking about three completely different things. And I’ve barely absorbed what I was supposed to be reading.

That’s the bit that’s causing problems, I think. Not so much that I can’t sit still or that I’m doing rash things — it’s that my attention just sort of disappears on me.

Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…\n\nSorry. Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.\n\nShe’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're easily distracted by other things around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less boredom, maybe? I mean, some of the stuff is boring, sure — marking essays isn’t exactly thrilling — but I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and then within ten minutes I realise I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s like I’m thinking about three other things at once, not even always because something in the room distracted me. Sometimes nothing has happened, really. I’m just… not taking in what’s right in front of me, and then I sort of come back and realise I’ve lost the thread completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more because you have trouble paying attention or because you have trouble with multiple things at once, like organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention side of it, yeah. I mean, not that I’m brilliantly organized or anything — my desk at school can get a bit… archaeological — but the thing that really made me come in is that I can sit down with essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work, and then within about ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’ll be thinking about three other things, like some email I need to send or something a student said earlier, and then I notice I’ve barely taken in any of what’s actually in front of me. So I’m not sure it’s really impulsivity or juggling loads of things at once. It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a need to be constantly active, like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say I feel like I physically can’t slow down, not in a… pacing-round-the-room sort of way.\n\nI mean, teaching does keep you on your feet a lot, but that’s just the job, isn’t it? You’re always going from one classroom thing to another, and then someone knocks on the door about a trip form or a printer not working — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I suppose I haven’t really noticed a constant need to be active like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I’d have to think about what you mean by “can’t slow down.”\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant periods of feeling sad, empty, or depressed that lasted for more than a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “this lasted weeks and I was depressed” sort of way.\n\nI mean, I get worn down sometimes, especially around exam periods or when the marking piles up, and then I can feel a bit flat, but that feels more like being tired and overloaded than anything else. Though I suppose it’s hard to tell sometimes, because school terms sort of blur together — you get to the end of a half-term and everyone’s walking around like a ghost, don’t they?\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had a significant spell of feeling sad or empty for weeks like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are the main problems you're experiencing, or are there other issues like restlessness or impulsivity that are equally or more troubling to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the focus and following through are the main things, really. I wouldn’t say restlessness or impulsivity are equally troubling — at least not what’s bringing me here.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll sit down with, say, a stack of essays or some curriculum document, and I genuinely mean to get through it. I’ve cleared the time, I’ve got my coffee, sometimes I’ve even got the nice pen out, which is ridiculous because it makes no difference — and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page and thinking about three completely different things. And I’ve barely absorbed what I was supposed to be reading.\n\nThat’s the bit that’s causing problems, I think. Not so much that I can’t sit still or that I’m doing rash things — it’s that my attention just sort of disappears on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I wouldn’t say restlessness or impulsivity are equally troubling — at least not what’s bringing me here.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll sit down with, say, a stack of essays or some curriculum document, and I genuinely mean to get through it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, really. I don’t think of myself as especially fidgety, but then I suppose I might do things without noticing — like clicking a pen or shifting in the chair. Although everyone does that a bit, don’t they?

At school, if I’m sitting through a long meeting, I might feel a bit… impatient, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it restless. Half the time in meetings I’m also thinking about whether I remembered to email someone about a display board, or whether Year 10 are going to behave after lunch, which is probably not what you asked.

So, no, I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless when I’m sitting still. Not in a way that stands out to me.

Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're easily distracted by other things around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less boredom, maybe? I mean, some of the stuff is boring, sure — marking essays isn’t exactly thrilling — but I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and then within ten minutes I realise I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s like I’m thinking about three other things at once, not even always because something in the room distracted me. I suppose I might shift in my chair or tap a pen sometimes, but then teaching is a bit like that anyway—you’re up, you’re down, you’re moving between desks, and the chairs in the staffroom are terrible, so everyone fidgets there.\n\nSorry, that’s not really the question. Sitting still… I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing, no. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a constant way, anyway. I mean, teaching is full of people talking over each other a bit—students especially, obviously—and in staff meetings everyone sort of jumps in before the topic changes, because otherwise you lose your chance.\n\nBut personally… I don’t think I’m known for interrupting. At least, no one’s really said that to me. I might occasionally jump in if I think I know where someone’s going, but I’m not sure that’s different from normal conversation. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Organizing tasks… I’m not sure, because teaching is kind of one long list of tasks, isn’t it? Lessons, marking, emails, forms, parents’ evenings—though parents’ evening is a whole separate thing, the way the timetable gets printed and then someone changes rooms at the last minute…\n\nSorry. Um, I suppose I’d need to know what you mean by trouble organizing. I use planners and lists like everyone does, but whether I’m worse at it than other people, I don’t really know. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I can get things done, generally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish things you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one probably does fit. Not, like, I abandon everything halfway through, because I do start things and I can get a lot of it done.\n\nBut if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll start with the first one and maybe do most of it, and then somehow the final bit just… doesn’t happen. Like sending the file, or putting it on the shared calendar, or printing the handouts. And then someone will say, “Did you send that?” and I’ll realize I got all the way up to the last step and just never actually finished it.\n\nIt’s annoying, because it looks careless, and I don’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience strong emotions such as anxiety, anger, or excitement?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that one. I mean, everyone gets anxious or annoyed or excited, don’t they? Especially in school, because one minute you’re dealing with a class that’s gone completely sideways and then the bell goes and suddenly you’re meant to be calm in front of the next group.\n\nBut strong emotions… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me, or not something I’ve particularly noticed as unusual. I can get stressed, obviously, but I don’t know if it’s more than other people. What do you mean by strong, like losing my temper, or just feeling it intensely?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks have been present for most of your life, or is this a relatively recent development?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, yeah. Not just recent.\n\nI mean, I remember in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” which is such a teacher phrase, isn’t it? I probably write versions of that now, actually, which is a bit awkward. And there were comments about me not finishing work unless someone was right there keeping an eye on me.\n\nMy parents used to find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So… yeah, I don’t think this suddenly started as an adult.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused you significant problems or stress in your daily life, beyond just the frustration you've mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose if I’m being honest, it has gone past just “oh, that’s annoying.”\n\nAt work it’s become a proper issue. My department chair’s put me on a support plan, which was horrible to hear, because I do care about the job. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I won’t bother.” It’s more that I’ll do most of something and then miss the bit that actually makes it useful to anyone else. And then it reflects badly on me, obviously.\n\nAnd at home too, yeah. My wife’s started taking over the bills and reminders, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them. Which is fair, I suppose, but it feels… not great. Like being managed a bit. But she’s not wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks caused problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with my wife, I think. I mean, I’ve got friends where I’m probably a bit flaky with messages or whatever, but the real strain is at home.\n\nShe’s taken over the bills and reminders because she basically can’t count on me to keep track of them, which… I get why, but it doesn’t feel great. It makes me feel a bit useless, honestly, like I’m another thing she has to manage.\n\nAnd work has sort of spilled into that too, because being put on a support plan by my department chair has been stressful, and I bring that home. So yes, it has caused problems, not like huge dramatic arguments every day, but definitely tension and this sense that people can’t rely on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more related to not paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… it does feel like people would describe it as careless mistakes, but I don’t sit there feeling careless, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look back at something and think, “How did I miss that?” but whether that’s not paying attention to detail or just rushing or my brain being somewhere else… I don’t know. Sorry, that’s probably not a very neat answer.\n\nDoctor: When you start a task, do you usually have trouble seeing it through to the end because you lose interest or because your mind wanders away from the task?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that my mind wanders, really. I don’t usually start off thinking, “I can’t be bothered with this.” Like with essays or a curriculum document, I’ll sit down properly, genuinely intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three other things.\n\nSometimes I’ve technically been looking at the words, but none of it’s gone in. And then I have to go back and reread it, which is frustrating because it’s not even that I chose to stop. My brain’s just sort of… wandered off without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're easily distracted by other things around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less boredom, maybe? I mean, some of the stuff is boring, sure — marking essays isn’t exactly thrilling — but I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and then within ten minutes I realise I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s like I’m thinking about three other things at once, not even always because something in the room distracted me. Sometimes nothing has happened, really. I’m just… not taking in what’s right in front of me, and then I sort of come back and realise I’ve lost the thread completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are more because you have trouble paying attention or because you have trouble with multiple things at once, like organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention side of it, yeah. I mean, not that I’m brilliantly organized or anything — my desk at school can get a bit… archaeological — but the thing that really made me come in is that I can sit down with essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to work, and then within about ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’ll be thinking about three other things, like some email I need to send or something a student said earlier, and then I notice I’ve barely taken in any of what’s actually in front of me. So I’m not sure it’s really impulsivity or juggling loads of things at once. It feels more like my attention just slips away, even when I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a need to be constantly active, like you can't slow down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say I feel like I physically can’t slow down, not in a… pacing-round-the-room sort of way.\n\nI mean, teaching does keep you on your feet a lot, but that’s just the job, isn’t it? You’re always going from one classroom thing to another, and then someone knocks on the door about a trip form or a printer not working — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I suppose I haven’t really noticed a constant need to be active like that. It’s more… I don’t know, I’d have to think about what you mean by “can’t slow down.”\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant periods of feeling sad, empty, or depressed that lasted for more than a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “this lasted weeks and I was depressed” sort of way.\n\nI mean, I get worn down sometimes, especially around exam periods or when the marking piles up, and then I can feel a bit flat, but that feels more like being tired and overloaded than anything else. Though I suppose it’s hard to tell sometimes, because school terms sort of blur together — you get to the end of a half-term and everyone’s walking around like a ghost, don’t they?\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve had a significant spell of feeling sad or empty for weeks like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks are the main problems you're experiencing, or are there other issues like restlessness or impulsivity that are equally or more troubling to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the focus and following through are the main things, really. I wouldn’t say restlessness or impulsivity are equally troubling — at least not what’s bringing me here.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll sit down with, say, a stack of essays or some curriculum document, and I genuinely mean to get through it. I’ve cleared the time, I’ve got my coffee, sometimes I’ve even got the nice pen out, which is ridiculous because it makes no difference — and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page and thinking about three completely different things. And I’ve barely absorbed what I was supposed to be reading.\n\nThat’s the bit that’s causing problems, I think. Not so much that I can’t sit still or that I’m doing rash things — it’s that my attention just sort of disappears on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think of myself as especially fidgety, but then I suppose I might do things without noticing — like clicking a pen or shifting in the chair. Although everyone does that a bit, don’t they?\n\nAt school, if I’m sitting through a long meeting, I might feel a bit… impatient, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it restless. Half the time in meetings I’m also thinking about whether I remembered to email someone about a display board, or whether Year 10 are going to behave after lunch, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless when I’m sitting still. Not in a way that stands out to me."
